### linear algebra & optimisation (W08)

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ArrayDot` | added | 6 | ArrayDot[a, b, k] contracts the last k indices of a with the first k of b; ArrayDot[a, b, {{s1, t1}, ...}] contracts index si of a with index ti of b (remaining indices of a, then of b); exact, symbolic and numeric arrays |
| `Band` | added | 17 | Band[start], Band[start, end], Band[start, end \| Automatic, step] in SparseArray rules: Band[...] -> v repeats v along the diagonal to the edge (or end); -> {v1, ...} fills successive positions (cycling when an end is given); -> array inserts a block; -> {a1, ...} places blocks along the diagonal (cyclically up to an explicit end, clipped at the edge); negative positions count from the end; Band itself is inert |
| `BayesianMaximization` | added | 8 | BayesianMaximization[f, spec] gives a BayesianMaximizationObject: Gaussian-process Bayesian optimisation (RBF kernel with the length scale chosen by marginal likelihood, expected-improvement or improvement-probability acquisition over random candidates) over an Interval, Rectangle/Cuboid, a list of numeric configurations or a sampler function; options MaxIterations (default 100), AssumeDeterministic, InitialEvaluationHistory, Method, RandomSeeding (default 1234, deterministic); results are stochastic like Wolfram's and the GP model differs, so configurations differ in detail; the "PredictorFunction" property raises |
| `BayesianMaximizationObject` | added | 3 | result of BayesianMaximization; obj["MaximumConfiguration"], ["MaximumValue"], ["EvaluationHistory"], ["ObjectiveFunction"], ["Method"], ["NextConfiguration"], ["Properties"] and lists of properties |
| `BayesianMinimization` | added | 11 | BayesianMinimization[f, spec] gives a BayesianMinimizationObject: Gaussian-process Bayesian optimisation (RBF kernel with the length scale chosen by marginal likelihood, expected-improvement or improvement-probability acquisition over random candidates) over an Interval, Rectangle/Cuboid, a list of numeric configurations or a sampler function; options MaxIterations (default 100), AssumeDeterministic, InitialEvaluationHistory, Method, RandomSeeding (default 1234, deterministic); results are stochastic like Wolfram's and the GP model differs, so configurations differ in detail; the "PredictorFunction" property raises |
| `BayesianMinimizationObject` | added | 3 | result of BayesianMinimization; obj["MinimumConfiguration"], ["MinimumValue"], ["EvaluationHistory"], ["ObjectiveFunction"], ["Method"], ["NextConfiguration"], ["Properties"] and lists of properties |
| `BinaryDistance` | added | 6 | BinaryDistance[u, v] = Boole[!(u == v)]: 0 for equal expressions (1 == 1. counts as equal), 1 for different ones; Boole[u != v] when equality cannot be decided |
| `BlockDiagonalMatrix` | added | 7 | BlockDiagonalMatrix[{d1, d2, ...}] the block diagonal matrix with square blocks di ({} blocks are 0 x 0); BlockDiagonalMatrix[mat] accepts a (block diagonal) square matrix; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `BlockLowerTriangularMatrix` | added | 3 | BlockLowerTriangularMatrix[lmat] for a square matrix (any square matrix is block lower triangular with a single block, as in Wolfram); MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `BlockUpperTriangularMatrix` | added | 3 | BlockUpperTriangularMatrix[umat] for a square matrix (any square matrix is block upper triangular with a single block, as in Wolfram); MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `BrayCurtisDistance` | added | 3 | BrayCurtisDistance[u, v] = Total[Abs[u - v]]/Total[Abs[u + v]] |
| `BunchKaufmanDecomposition` | added | 6 | {l, b, p} with p.m.Transpose[p] == l.b.ConjugateTranspose[l] (Hermitian m) or l.b.Transpose[l] (complex symmetric m): Bunch-Kaufman diagonal pivoting (alpha = (1 + Sqrt[17])/8), l unit lower triangular, b block diagonal with 1x1 and 2x2 blocks, p a permutation matrix; exact or machine numeric matrices (symbolic matrices raise); pivot choices can differ from Wolfram's on ties; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `CanberraDistance` | added | 3 | CanberraDistance[u, v] = Total[Abs[u - v]/(Abs[u] + Abs[v])] (terms with u_i = v_i = 0 are 0) |
| `ChessboardDistance` | added | 4 | ChessboardDistance[u, v] = Max[Abs[u - v]] (Chebyshev distance) |
| `CompanionMatrix` | added | 12 | CompanionMatrix[{c0, ..., c(n-1)}] (implicit monic x^n term) and CompanionMatrix[poly, x] (normalised by the leading coefficient): ones on the subdiagonal and -c_i down the last column; CompanionMatrix[..., dir] with dir = Right (default), Bottom (transpose), Top (-c(n-1), ..., -c0 along the first row) or Left (its transpose); TargetStructure -> "Dense" \| "Sparse" accepted (dense result) |
| `ConicOptimization` | added | 3 | ConicOptimization[f, cons, vars] for a linear objective with conic constraints (NormCone, SemidefiniteCone, ExponentialCone, PowerCone, quadratic inequalities): linear problems are solved exactly (results as machine numbers), convex quadratic ones by active-set enumeration, the rest with SciPy SLSQP after a sampled convexity check (a visibly non-convex objective or constraint raises); matrix forms are not supported; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `ConvexOptimization` | added | 4 | ConvexOptimization[f, cons, vars] for a convex objective and constraints: linear problems are solved exactly (results as machine numbers), convex quadratic ones by active-set enumeration, the rest with SciPy SLSQP after a sampled convexity check (a visibly non-convex objective or constraint raises); matrix forms are not supported; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `CoreNilpotentDecomposition` | added | 7 | {t, c, n} with m == t.BlockDiagonalMatrix[{c, n}].Inverse[t], c invertible and n nilpotent; exact matrices use t = {pivot columns of m^k, integer null-space basis of m^k} (k the index of m), as Wolfram; invertible or nilpotent m give t = IdentityMatrix and {} for the empty part; machine matrices use an SVD basis (t is not unique); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `CorrelationDistance` | added | 3 | CorrelationDistance[u, v] = 1 - (u - Mean[u]).Conjugate[v - Mean[v]]/(Norm[u - Mean[u]] Norm[v - Mean[v]]) |
| `CosineDistance` | added | 4 | CosineDistance[u, v] = 1 - u.Conjugate[v]/(Norm[u] Norm[v]); a zero vector raises |
| `DiceDissimilarity` | added | 5 | DiceDissimilarity[u, v] = (n10 + n01)/(2 n11 + n10 + n01) for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `DistanceFunction` | added | 6 | option name DistanceFunction -> f (Automatic or a two-argument distance) for Nearest, DistanceMatrix, WarpingDistance, FeatureDistance, ...; an inert symbol |
| `DistanceMatrix` | added | 6 | DistanceMatrix[{u1, ...}] and DistanceMatrix[{u1, ...}, {v1, ...}] matrix of distances (Euclidean for numeric data, EditDistance for strings, JaccardDissimilarity for Boolean vectors) or DistanceFunction -> f; associations use their values; PerformanceGoal is accepted (results are always computed exactly or at input precision) |
| `DrazinInverse` | added | 7 | the Drazin inverse t.BlockDiagonalMatrix[{Inverse[c], 0}].Inverse[t] from the core-nilpotent decomposition (the inverse for invertible m, zero for nilpotent m); exact, symbolic (generic) or machine square matrices; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `EigenvalueDecomposition` | added | 5 | {s, d} with m == s.d.Inverse[s]: s has the eigenvectors (Eigenvectors[m]) as columns and d the eigenvalues on the diagonal, ordered as Eigensystem; rational eigenvectors are scaled to coprime integers as Wolfram does; a non-diagonalizable matrix raises; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `EuclideanDistance` | added | 6 | EuclideanDistance[u, v] = Sqrt[Total[Abs[u - v]^2]] for numeric or symbolic vectors (Abs[u - v] for scalars); results keep the precision of the input |
| `FeatureDistance` | added | 4 | FeatureDistance[e1, e2, extractor] distance between extractor[e1] and extractor[e2] (EuclideanDistance for numeric features, or DistanceFunction -> f); extractor is any function (e.g. a pure Function or Identity); FeatureDistance[extractor][e1, e2] operator form; FeatureExtractorFunction objects (machine-learned extractors from FeatureExtraction) are not available and raise |
| `FindArgMax` | added | 3 | the local maximiser of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a list of values for vars ({x, x0} starting points and {x, x0, xmin, xmax} accepted); linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (a local search from the starting point (default 1)); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `FindArgMin` | added | 3 | the local minimiser of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a list of values for vars ({x, x0} starting points and {x, x0, xmin, xmax} accepted); linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (a local search from the starting point (default 1)); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `FindMatrixGameStrategies` | added | 4 | Nash equilibria of a matrix game as a list of strategy profiles: pure equilibria first, then mixed ones, each sorted; two-player games use exact support enumeration (all equilibria of a non-degenerate game; for degenerate games the extreme equilibria with equal-size supports), n-player games give the pure equilibria only with "Pure" (mixed n-player equilibria raise); FindMatrixGameStrategies[game, "Pure" \| "Mixed"] restricts the kind; the Method option is not supported |
| `FindMaxValue` | added | 3 | the local maximum value of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a machine number; linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (a local search from the starting point (default 1)); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `FindMinValue` | added | 3 | the local minimum value of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a machine number; linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (a local search from the starting point (default 1)); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `FindShortestTour` | added | 7 | FindShortestTour[{p1, ...}] gives {length, tour} for the shortest closed tour (starting and ending at 1); FindShortestTour[pts, j, k] the shortest path from pj to pk through all points; points are vectors (EuclideanDistance, exact lengths for exact points) or strings (EditDistance), or use DistanceFunction -> f; exact (Held-Karp dynamic programming) for up to 12 points, a 2-opt/Or-opt local search beyond that (not guaranteed optimal, like Wolfram's heuristics); the direction of an optimal tour and the choice among equally short tours can differ from Wolfram's; graphs and GeoPositions raise |
| `FindTreeGameStrategies` | added | 4 | pure-strategy solutions of a TreeGame by backward induction: at each action node every action that is optimal for the moving player under some tie-breaking below it is kept, and all combinations are listed (this reproduces Wolfram's results, including its handling of ties), each as <\|player -> <\|node -> probabilities\|>\|> with nodes as child-index paths ({} the root), players in order of appearance (chance players get <\|\|>), sorted; payoff vectors are matched to the non-chance players in order of appearance; as in Wolfram, information sets are ignored (each node solved separately) |
| `FitRegularization` | added | 3 | option name (Wolfram: {"Tikhonov", l}, {"LASSO", l}, ... regularisation for Fit/FindFit); MikoshiLang's Fit and FindFit do not accept it and raise an unknown-option error |
| `FrobeniusDecomposition` | added | 3 | {s, c} with m == s.c.Inverse[s] and c the Frobenius (rational canonical) form: block diagonal with companion matrices (ones below the diagonal, coefficients in the last column) of the invariant factors, each dividing the next; exact numeric and symbolic matrices (generic case) via cyclic Krylov decomposition; s is not unique and can differ from Wolfram's; Modulus and inexact input raise; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `FrobeniusReduce` | added | 6 | the Frobenius (rational canonical) form of a square matrix (unique; FrobeniusDecomposition[m][[2]]); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `GameActionLabels` | added | 3 | option of MatrixGame and MatrixGamePlot: one list of action labels per player |
| `GamePlayerLabels` | added | 3 | option of MatrixGame and MatrixGamePlot: a list of player labels |
| `GeometricOptimization` | added | 4 | GeometricOptimization[f, cons, vars] for a posynomial objective with posynomial <= monomial and monomial == monomial constraints in positive variables: solved as the convex problem in u = Log[x] (SciPy SLSQP); matrix forms are not supported; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `HadamardMatrix` | added | 8 | HadamardMatrix[n] (n a power of 2): the normalised (orthogonal, symmetric) Walsh-Hadamard matrix with entries +-1/Sqrt[n], rows in sequency order (Method -> "Sequency", default), Sylvester order ("BitComplement") or Paley/dyadic order ("GrayCode"); WorkingPrecision -> p gives numeric entries; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `HermitianMatrix` | added | 4 | HermitianMatrix[hmat] validates a Hermitian matrix; HermitianMatrix[{{h11, h12, ...}, {h22, ...}, ...}] or the lower rows {{h11}, {h21, h22}, ...} fill in the conjugate entries; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `JaccardDissimilarity` | added | 4 | JaccardDissimilarity[u, v] = (n10 + n01)/(n11 + n10 + n01) for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `JordanMatrix` | added | 6 | JordanMatrix[l, n] n x n Jordan block (l on the diagonal, 1 on the superdiagonal); JordanMatrix[l, n, Right] puts the ones below the diagonal (the transpose); l must be a scalar; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `JordanReduce` | added | 4 | the Jordan normal form j of a square matrix (exact via SymPy, blocks ordered by eigenvalue real part then imaginary part); machine matrices must be diagonalizable and give the diagonal matrix of Eigenvalues[m] (decreasing absolute value); Wolfram's block order can differ for exact matrices; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `KarhunenLoeveDecomposition` | added | 9 | KarhunenLoeveDecomposition[{a1, ..., an}] gives {{b1, ..., bn}, m}: the rows of m are the unit eigenvectors of the covariance matrix of the (flattened) arrays ai in order of decreasing eigenvalue (sign: largest component positive) and {b1, ...} == m.{a1, ...}; Standardized -> True first shifts each ai to zero mean; KarhunenLoeveDecomposition[{b1, ...}, m] inverts the transform (missing b's are 0); machine-precision results |
| `KnapsackSolve` | added | 8 | KnapsackSolve[{cost1, ...}, capacity] maximises the number of items, KnapsackSolve[{{payoff, cost}, ...}, capacity] and {payoff, cost, maxcount} items (or "Payoff"/"Cost"/"MaxCount" rules or associations) maximise the total payoff with total cost <= capacity, giving the item counts (an association of items gives an association); exact integer programming (branch and bound on the exact simplex), ties broken by the smaller total cost; the constraints can be maxcost, {maxcost}, {maxpayoff, maxcost}, {maxpayoff, maxcost, maxcount} or an association of "MaxTotalPayoff", "MaxTotalCost", "MaxTotalCount"; Quantity payoffs/costs raise |
| `LDLDecomposition` | added | 8 | {l, d} with m == l.d.ConjugateTranspose[l], l unit lower triangular and d diagonal, for a Hermitian (or real symmetric) matrix, exact, symbolic or machine; a zero pivot (no LDL factorisation without pivoting) raises (use BunchKaufmanDecomposition); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `LinearFractionalOptimization` | added | 3 | minimise (a.x + b)/(c.x + d) subject to linear constraints (the denominator must be positive on the feasible set): exact Charnes-Cooper transformation to a linear program, branch and bound for integer variables; matrix form [{a, b, c, d}, {a1, b1}, ...]; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `LinearOptimization` | added | 8 | LinearOptimization[f, cons, vars] gives the minimiser rules; LinearOptimization[c, {a, b}] (a.x + b >= 0) and [c, {a, b}, {aeq, beq}] (aeq.x + beq == 0) give the vector; domains {Integers, Reals, ...}; exact rational simplex (Bland's rule) so exact input gives exact results, branch and bound for integer variables (integer values exact, the others machine numbers as Wolfram); vertex choice among multiple optima can differ from Wolfram's; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `LinearSolveFunction` | added | 3 | LinearSolve[m] gives LinearSolveFunction[{r, c}, m] (the stored data is the matrix itself, not Wolfram's factorisation data); f[b] solves m.x == b for a vector or matrix b like LinearSolve[m, b] |
| `LossFunction` | added | 3 | option name (Wolfram: the loss for NetTrain/Predict/Classify); MikoshiLang's machine-learning functions do not accept it |
| `LowerTriangularMatrix` | added | 3 | LowerTriangularMatrix[m] validates a square lower triangular matrix (entries above the diagonal must be zero, else a clear error); MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `ManhattanDistance` | added | 5 | ManhattanDistance[u, v] = Total[Abs[u - v]] |
| `MatchingDissimilarity` | added | 3 | MatchingDissimilarity[u, v] = (n10 + n01)/Length[u] for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `MatrixGame` | added | 21 | MatrixGame[{{a11, ...}, ...}] (two-player zero-sum) or MatrixGame[p] with p of dimensions {m1, ..., mn, n} gives MatrixGame[payoff array, options..., "Validity" -> {True}]; options GameActionLabels and GamePlayerLabels; properties "PayoffArray", "ComponentPayoffArray", "GameActionLabels", "GamePlayerLabels", "Dataset" (Dataset of payoff vectors by action labels) and "Plot" (MatrixGamePlot); "TreeGame" and SparseArray/rule specifications raise |
| `MatrixGamePayoff` | added | 3 | MatrixGamePayoff[game, {s1, ..., sn}] gives the expected payoff of each player for mixed strategies si (probability vectors; also {1 -> s1, ...} rules or <\|label -> s\|>, and lists of profiles); MatrixGamePayoff[game, profile, "Expectation" \| "Variance"]; exact for exact strategies; joint group strategies and the distribution/simulation properties raise |
| `MatrixGamePlot` | added | 3 | MatrixGamePlot[game] gives Graphics[...]: a grid of payoff cells (rows: the first Ceiling[n/2] players' action combinations, columns: the rest), each split into one coloured strip per player with the payoff as Text, and action/player labels (GameActionLabels, GamePlayerLabels options); Graphics options are passed through; PlotLayout, ColorFunction and strategy thickness are not supported (raise) |
| `NArgMax` | added | 3 | the global maximiser of f (optionally {f, cons}) (a single variable gives a number, a list of variables a list); linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (multi-start, so a global optimum is likely but not guaranteed for non-convex problems); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `NArgMin` | added | 3 | the global minimiser of f (optionally {f, cons}) (a single variable gives a number, a list of variables a list); linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (multi-start, so a global optimum is likely but not guaranteed for non-convex problems); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `NMaxValue` | added | 3 | the global maximum value of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a machine number; linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (multi-start, so a global optimum is likely but not guaranteed for non-convex problems); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `NMinValue` | added | 3 | the global minimum value of f (optionally {f, cons}) as a machine number; linear problems are solved exactly (rational simplex, branch and bound for integer variables), convex quadratic ones by exact active-set enumeration, others with SciPy SLSQP (multi-start, so a global optimum is likely but not guaranteed for non-convex problems); constraints may be equations, inequalities, vector inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual, ...), x \[Element] Integers and {x, ...} \[Element] Disk/Ball/Rectangle/Cuboid; infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `NormFunction` | added | 3 | option name (Wolfram: the norm minimised by FindFit/Fit); MikoshiLang's FindFit and Fit do not accept it and raise an unknown-option error |
| `Normalized` | added | 3 | option of OrthogonalMatrixQ and UnitaryMatrixQ (Normalized -> False only requires orthogonal columns/rows) |
| `NormalizedSquaredEuclideanDistance` | added | 3 | NormalizedSquaredEuclideanDistance[u, v] = Norm[(u - Mean[u]) - (v - Mean[v])]^2/(2 (Norm[u - Mean[u]]^2 + Norm[v - Mean[v]]^2)) |
| `OrderedSchurDecomposition` | added | 11 | {q, t} with m == q.t.ConjugateTranspose[q], t (block) upper triangular with eigenvalues ordered by ord (default "DecreasingAbs"; also IncreasingAbs, Decreasing/IncreasingRe, ...AbsRe, ...AbsIm); RealBlockDiagonalForm -> True (default) keeps 2x2 real blocks for complex pairs of a real matrix; {m, a} gives the generalized decomposition {q, s, p, t} with m == q.s.ConjugateTranspose[p], a == q.t.ConjugateTranspose[p] ordered by the generalized eigenvalues; LAPACK reordering (trexc/tgexc) of SciPy's Schur/QZ forms; numeric matrices only (exact input is evaluated numerically); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `OrthogonalMatrix` | added | 3 | OrthogonalMatrix[omat] validates a (square, tall or wide) orthogonal matrix: Transpose[o].o (or o.Transpose[o]) is the identity; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `ParametricConvexOptimization` | added | 4 | ParametricConvexOptimization[f, cons, vars, pars] gives ParametricFunction[...]; applying it to parameter values solves ConvexOptimization[f, cons, vars] with the parameters substituted (each call re-solves the problem; Wolfram's precompiled solver data are not reproduced); constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `PermutationMatrix` | added | 7 | PermutationMatrix[permv] has a 1 in column permv[[i]] of row i; also from Cycles[...] (size = largest moved point), i <-> j, or a permutation matrix (validated); PermutationMatrix[perm, n] pads to n x n; Det is Signature[permv]; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `PfaffianDet` | added | 9 | the Pfaffian of an antisymmetric matrix (Pf[m]^2 == Det[m], 0 for odd dimension): exact and machine numeric matrices by skew-symmetric (Parlett-Reid style) elimination with pivoting, symbolic matrices by expansion along the first row; Method -> Automatic, "ParlettReid", "Householder", "Hessenberg" and "Pauli" give the same value, "Det" gives Sqrt[Det[m]] (sign not conformed, as Wolfram); a non-antisymmetric matrix raises |
| `PolarDecomposition` | added | 5 | {u, p} with m == u.p, u with orthonormal columns (or rows) and p = MatrixPower[ConjugateTranspose[m].m, 1/2] positive semidefinite, via the SVD for machine matrices; exact and arbitrary-precision matrices raise (use N[m]); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `PopovDecomposition` | added | 14 | PopovDecomposition[m] or PopovDecomposition[m, x] gives {u, p} with u unimodular (Det[u] a nonzero constant), u.m == p and p the Popov form of the univariate polynomial matrix m: each nonzero row's pivot (last entry of highest degree) lies in its own column, pivot degrees increase (ties: leftmost pivot first), other entries of a pivot column have lower degree, and zero rows come last.  The form is unique up to row scaling; here pivots are made monic (Wolfram may normalise rows differently, e.g. to integer coefficients).  Exact rational, complex-rational and symbolic coefficients (then x must be given) work exactly; Modulus -> prime works over GF(p); approximate coefficients are converted to exact rationals, decomposed exactly and returned as machine numbers. |
| `PrincipalComponents` | added | 4 | PrincipalComponents[matrix] transforms the rows (observations; columns are variables) to unscaled principal components: the centred data times the unit eigenvectors of the covariance matrix in order of decreasing variance; Method -> "Correlation" standardises the columns first; machine numeric data (exact numbers are evaluated numerically); the sign of each component is normalised so that the largest eigenvector component is positive, which can differ from Wolfram's sign choice; symbolic and arbitrary-precision data raise |
| `QuadraticOptimization` | added | 5 | minimise a convex quadratic objective subject to linear constraints: QuadraticOptimization[f, cons, vars] or [{q, c}, {a, b}, {aeq, beq}] (1/2 x.q.x + c.x); exact active-set enumeration (up to 12 inequalities, results as machine numbers like Wolfram), SciPy beyond that; a non-convex objective raises; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `RankDecomposition` | added | 6 | {c, f} with m == c.f and c, f of full rank MatrixRank[m]: exact and symbolic matrices give c = the pivot columns of m and f = the non-zero rows of RowReduce[m] (as Wolfram); machine matrices give c with orthonormal columns (SVD-based, the factorisation is not unique); the zero matrix raises; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `RealBlockDiagonalForm` | added | 4 | option of OrderedSchurDecomposition (and Wolfram's SchurDecomposition): True keeps real 2x2 blocks for complex-conjugate eigenvalue pairs of a real matrix, False gives a complex upper triangular form |
| `Regularization` | added | 3 | option name (Wolfram: regularisation of divergent sums, "Abel", "Borel", "Cesaro", "Dirichlet", "Euler"); Sum raises when it is given |
| `RobustConvexOptimization` | added | 3 | RobustConvexOptimization[f, ForAll[pars, cond, cons], vars] and [f, cons, vars, pars] (parameter bounds among cons): the constraints (and objective, through an epigraph variable) must hold for every parameter value in a box given by bounds or Interval memberships; when the objective and constraint functions are affine in each parameter or convex in the parameters (sampled Hessian check) the worst case is attained at the box vertices, which are all enforced; other uncertainty sets or parameter dependence raise; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `RogersTanimotoDissimilarity` | added | 3 | RogersTanimotoDissimilarity[u, v] = 2 (n10 + n01)/(n11 + n00 + 2 (n10 + n01)) for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `RulePlot` | added | 5 | RulePlot[CellularAutomaton[n \| {n, k} \| {n, k, r}]] gives Graphics of the rule icons (each neighbourhood, in decreasing order, above the new centre cell; colour v drawn as GrayLevel[1 - v/(k-1)]) and RulePlot[TuringMachine[n \| {n, s, k}]] the icons of each {state, colour} case (head state as a number above the cell, new colour, state and move below); other systems (and 2D rules) raise; the drawing style differs from Wolfram's |
| `RussellRaoDissimilarity` | added | 3 | RussellRaoDissimilarity[u, v] = (n10 + n01 + n00)/Length[u] for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `SecondOrderConeOptimization` | added | 3 | SecondOrderConeOptimization[f, cons, vars] for a linear objective with second-order-cone (Norm[...] <= ...) constraints: linear problems are solved exactly (results as machine numbers), convex quadratic ones by active-set enumeration, the rest with SciPy SLSQP after a sampled convexity check (a visibly non-convex objective or constraint raises); matrix forms are not supported; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `SemidefiniteOptimization` | added | 3 | SemidefiniteOptimization[f, cons, vars] for a linear objective with linear matrix inequalities (VectorGreaterEqual[{m, 0}, {"SemidefiniteCone", n}]): linear problems are solved exactly (results as machine numbers), convex quadratic ones by active-set enumeration, the rest with SciPy SLSQP after a sampled convexity check (a visibly non-convex objective or constraint raises); matrix forms are not supported; constraints as in NMinimize (equations, inequalities, vector inequalities, x \[Element] Integers); properties "PrimalMinimizer", "PrimalMinimizerRules", "PrimalMinimizerVector" and "PrimalMinimumValue" (dual properties raise); infeasible or unbounded problems raise |
| `SmithDecomposition` | added | 5 | {u, r, v} with u.m.v == r, u and v unimodular and r the Smith normal form (diagonal, non-negative, each entry dividing the next) for integer matrices; rational matrices are handled by clearing denominators (as Wolfram); the transformation matrices are not unique and can differ from Wolfram's; inexact, Gaussian-integer and symbolic matrices raise; TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `SmithReduce` | added | 5 | the Smith normal form r of an integer or rational matrix (SmithDecomposition[m][[2]], unique); TargetStructure is accepted, but MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the factors are always ordinary (dense) matrices |
| `SokalSneathDissimilarity` | added | 3 | SokalSneathDissimilarity[u, v] = 2 (n10 + n01)/(n11 + 2 (n10 + n01)) for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |
| `SquaredEuclideanDistance` | added | 3 | SquaredEuclideanDistance[u, v] = Total[Abs[u - v]^2] |
| `Standardized` | added | 4 | option of KarhunenLoeveDecomposition: True shifts each array to zero mean before the transform |
| `SymmetricMatrix` | added | 4 | SymmetricMatrix[smat] validates a symmetric matrix; SymmetricMatrix[{{s11, s12, ...}, {s22, ...}, ...}] or the lower-triangular rows {{s11}, {s21, s22}, ...} fill in the other triangle; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `TransformationMatrix` | added | 4 | TransformationMatrix[TransformationFunction[m]] gives the homogeneous matrix m; anything else stays unevaluated |
| `TuringMachine` | added | 9 | one-dimensional Turing machines: rule n, {n, s} or {n, s, k} (Wolfram numbering) or explicit rules {s, a} -> {s', a', offset} (patterns and conditions allowed; no matching rule leaves the configuration unchanged); initial conditions {s, {{a1, ...}, b}} (infinite tape with background b), {{s, x}, ...}, {{s, x, dx}, ...} and finite cyclic tapes {s, {a1, ...}}; TuringMachine[rule, init, t] gives the t + 1 steps {{s, x, dx}, tape} (infinite tapes padded to the region visited), TuringMachine[rule, init] and TuringMachine[rule][init] one step; multidimensional machines, offset ranges and repetitive backgrounds raise |
| `UnconstrainedParameters` | added | 3 | option name (Wolfram: parameters left free in GeometricScene); GeometricScene does not accept it here |
| `UnitaryMatrix` | added | 3 | UnitaryMatrix[umat] validates a unitary matrix: ConjugateTranspose[u].u (or u.ConjugateTranspose[u]) is the identity; MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `UpperTriangularMatrix` | added | 3 | UpperTriangularMatrix[m] validates a square upper triangular matrix (entries below the diagonal must be zero, else a clear error); MikoshiLang has no structured arrays, so the result is the ordinary matrix (Wolfram's Normal[...] or TargetStructure -> "Dense" form) and structured-array properties are not available |
| `VectorGreater` | added | 4 | VectorGreater[{x, y, ...}] (infix \[VectorGreater]): strict elementwise > comparison of numeric scalars/arrays, a scalar is broadcast against an array; VectorGreater[{x, y}, cone] uses the partial order of {"NonNegativeCone", n}, {"NormCone", n}, {"SemidefiniteCone", n}, "ExponentialCone", "DualExponentialCone" or {"PowerCone", a}; symbolic comparisons stay unevaluated (they are understood as constraints by the optimisers) |
| `VectorGreaterEqual` | added | 13 | VectorGreaterEqual[{x, y, ...}] (infix \[VectorGreaterEqual]): elementwise >= comparison of numeric scalars/arrays, a scalar is broadcast against an array; VectorGreaterEqual[{x, y}, cone] uses the partial order of {"NonNegativeCone", n}, {"NormCone", n}, {"SemidefiniteCone", n}, "ExponentialCone", "DualExponentialCone" or {"PowerCone", a}; symbolic comparisons stay unevaluated (they are understood as constraints by the optimisers) |
| `VectorLess` | added | 7 | VectorLess[{x, y, ...}] (infix \[VectorLess]): strict elementwise < comparison of numeric scalars/arrays, a scalar is broadcast against an array; VectorLess[{x, y}, cone] uses the partial order of {"NonNegativeCone", n}, {"NormCone", n}, {"SemidefiniteCone", n}, "ExponentialCone", "DualExponentialCone" or {"PowerCone", a}; symbolic comparisons stay unevaluated (they are understood as constraints by the optimisers) |
| `VectorLessEqual` | added | 4 | VectorLessEqual[{x, y, ...}] (infix \[VectorLessEqual]): elementwise <= comparison of numeric scalars/arrays, a scalar is broadcast against an array; VectorLessEqual[{x, y}, cone] uses the partial order of {"NonNegativeCone", n}, {"NormCone", n}, {"SemidefiniteCone", n}, "ExponentialCone", "DualExponentialCone" or {"PowerCone", a}; symbolic comparisons stay unevaluated (they are understood as constraints by the optimisers) |
| `VerifyMatrixGameStrategy` | added | 3 | True when the strategy profile {s1, ..., sn} is a Nash equilibrium of the matrix game (no player gains by deviating to any pure action; machine-number strategies use a 10^-9 tolerance); a list of profiles gives a list; symbolic strategies raise |
| `VerifyTreeGameStrategy` | added | 5 | True when the strategy profile <\|player -> <\|node -> probabilities\|>\|> is a subgame-perfect equilibrium of a perfect-information TreeGame (at every action node no action gives the moving player a higher expected payoff than the strategy, given the strategy below it; a 10^-9 tolerance for machine numbers); every non-chance action node must be specified; information sets are ignored; symbolic strategies raise |
| `WarpingCorrespondence` | added | 4 | WarpingCorrespondence[s1, s2] = {n, m}: the index lists of the optimal dynamic time warping path (same windows and DistanceFunction as WarpingDistance; ties prefer the diagonal step) |
| `WarpingDistance` | added | 6 | WarpingDistance[s1, s2] dynamic time warping distance (sum of element distances along the optimal warping path; EuclideanDistance for numbers and vectors, BinaryDistance/MatchingDissimilarity for Booleans, or DistanceFunction -> f); WarpingDistance[s1, s2, win] with win = r \| {"SlantedBand", r} \| {"Band", r} \| Automatic; the result is a machine real; the Parallelogram window and Method settings raise |
| `YuleDissimilarity` | added | 4 | YuleDissimilarity[u, v] = 2 n10 n01/(n11 n00 + n10 n01) for Boolean vectors (True/False or 0/1), where nij counts positions with u = i, v = j; 0/0 gives 0 |

### linear algebra (batch 3)

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Adjugate` | added | 6 | the adjugate (transposed cofactor matrix), Det[m] Inverse[m] for invertible m, defined for singular m too; exact (machine matrices are computed exactly from their binary values and rounded) |
| `AntihermitianMatrixQ` | added | 3 | m == -ConjugateTranspose[m] (exact; Tolerance / SameTest as for HermitianMatrixQ) |
| `AntisymmetricMatrixQ` | added | 4 | m == -Transpose[m] (exact; Tolerance / SameTest as for HermitianMatrixQ) |
| `ArrayRules` | added | 5 | ArrayRules[array] gives {pos -> value, ...} for the entries that differ from 0 (in lexicographic position order) followed by {_, _, ...} -> 0; ArrayRules[array, default]; works on explicit rectangular arrays (SparseArray evaluates to its dense array in MikoshiLang) |
| `BoxMatrix` | added | 5 | 1s in a box extending r index positions to each side: BoxMatrix[r] is (2r+1) x (2r+1) ones, BoxMatrix[r, w] (w x w, box centred), BoxMatrix[{r1, r2, ...}] and sizes {w1, ...}; integer radii only; an off-centre box (w - 2r - 1 odd) raises |
| `CauchyMatrix` | added | 4 | entries 1/(x_i + y_j) (CauchyMatrix[x] = CauchyMatrix[x, x]), returned as the dense matrix; a zero denominator raises |
| `ConjugateTranspose` | added | 12 | Conjugate[Transpose[m]] (and ConjugateTranspose[m, perm]); a vector or non-array raises like Transpose |
| `CrossMatrix` | added | 3 | 1s on the central row and column (central lines in higher dimensions) extending r positions: CrossMatrix[r] is (2r+1) x (2r+1), CrossMatrix[r, w], CrossMatrix[{r1, ...}]; integer radii only |
| `Det` | fixed | 43 | kernel builtin replaced in batch 3: non-square input raises a clear error (legacy raised SymPy's NonSquareMatrixError), a symbol leaves Det unevaluated (legacy raised TypeError), machine matrices use LAPACK, arbitrary-precision matrices keep their precision, symbolic determinants are expanded |
| `Diagonal` | added | 11 | Diagonal[m] = {m[[1,1]], m[[2,2]], ...} (rectangular matrices too); Diagonal[m, k] is the k-th superdiagonal (k < 0: subdiagonal); entries are returned unevaluated-as-given |
| `DiagonalizableMatrixQ` | added | 5 | m == s.d.Inverse[s] for a diagonal d (over the complex numbers): exact via SymPy; machine matrices test that the eigenvector matrix is well-conditioned (condition number < 10^8) |
| `DiamondMatrix` | added | 4 | 1s where the Manhattan distance from the centre is at most r: DiamondMatrix[r] is (2r+1) x (2r+1), DiamondMatrix[r, w]; integer radii only, equal radii per dimension |
| `Dot` | fixed | 10 | kernel builtin replaced in batch 3: any number of arguments and tensors of any rank (contracts the last index of each factor with the first of the next); symbolic factors leave Dot unevaluated (legacy raised); Dot[x] = x; incompatible shapes raise; infix a.b parses as Dot |
| `DotProduct` | fixed | 5 | MikoshiLang alias of Dot for two vectors (now the batch-3 Dot) |
| `Eigensystem` | added | 6 | {Eigenvalues[m], Eigenvectors[m]} with matching order (decreasing absolute value); Eigensystem[m, k] and UpTo[k]; generalized {m, a} raises |
| `FourierMatrix` | added | 4 | FourierMatrix[n]: entries Exp[2 Pi I (r - 1)(s - 1)/n]/Sqrt[n] (Wolfram's default FourierParameters {0, 1}); exact |
| `HankelMatrix` | added | 5 | HankelMatrix[n] (successive integers, zeros below the antidiagonal), HankelMatrix[c] (first column c, zeros below the antidiagonal), HankelMatrix[c, r] (first column c, last row r; r[[1]] is ignored and c[[-1]] used, as in Wolfram); always a dense matrix |
| `HermitianMatrixQ` | added | 6 | m == ConjugateTranspose[m] (exact zero test; Tolerance -> t for approximate matrices, default 1e-12 at machine precision; SameTest -> f); False for non-matrices |
| `HilbertMatrix` | added | 15 | entries 1/(i + j - 1); HilbertMatrix[n] and HilbertMatrix[{m, n}]; exact (use N for machine numbers) |
| `IndefiniteMatrixQ` | added | 5 | the quadratic form takes both signs (neither positive nor negative semidefinite); the quadratic form is Re[Conjugate[x].m.x], so only the Hermitian part (m + ConjugateTranspose[m])/2 matters (Wolfram); exact matrices use principal minors, machine matrices the eigenvalues of the Hermitian part with a tolerance of n*$MachineEpsilon*max\|eigenvalue\|; False for symbolic matrices unless every sign can be proved, and for non-matrices |
| `Inverse` | fixed | 23 | kernel builtin replaced in batch 3: a symbol leaves Inverse unevaluated (legacy raised), non-square and singular matrices raise clear errors, machine matrices use LAPACK, symbolic entries are cancelled |
| `JordanDecomposition` | added | 6 | {s, j} with m == s.j.Inverse[s] and j in Jordan normal form (exact, SymPy); a machine matrix must be numerically diagonalizable (eigenvector matrix condition number < 10^8) and gives {eigenvectors as columns, diagonal eigenvalues}; otherwise it raises. The order of the Jordan blocks and the scaling of s can differ from Wolfram's (the decomposition is not unique) |
| `LeastSquares` | added | 6 | the x minimising Norm[m.x - b] (minimum-norm solution when several exist, = PseudoInverse[m].b); b a vector or matrix; exact for exact input, LAPACK lstsq for machine input |
| `LeviCivitaTensor` | added | 11 | the rank-d totally antisymmetric tensor (Signature of the index list), as a dense array: LeviCivitaTensor[d] (Wolfram returns the equivalent SparseArray; Normal gives this array) and LeviCivitaTensor[d, List] |
| `LowerTriangularize` | added | 4 | zeros above the k-th diagonal (keeps m[[i,j]] with j - i <= k; default k = 0); rectangular matrices |
| `MatrixForm` | added | 4 | display wrapper: MatrixForm[expr] stays as MatrixForm[expr] and does not affect evaluation of expr (as in Wolfram, it is not a matrix); the text REPL prints it as an aligned grid (matrix_form_text) |
| `MatrixFunction` | added | 7 | MatrixFunction[f, m] for a square matrix and any scalar function f (symbol or pure function): exact via the Jordan form (SymPy analytic_func, derivatives of f on Jordan blocks); machine matrices via the eigendecomposition, which must be well-conditioned (else raises); f is evaluated by MikoshiLang |
| `MatrixMinimalPolynomial` | added | 6 | the monic polynomial p of least degree with p(m) == 0 (exact; Krylov dependence of I, m, m^2, ...); inexact matrices raise |
| `Minors` | added | 9 | Minors[m, k]: determinants of all k x k submatrices, rows and columns subsets in lexicographic order (Wolfram); Minors[m] = Minors[m, Min[Dimensions[m]] - 1]; Minors[m, k, f] applies f instead of Det; Minors[m, 0] = {{1}} |
| `NegativeDefiniteMatrixQ` | added | 3 | Re[Conjugate[x].m.x] < 0 for all non-zero x; the quadratic form is Re[Conjugate[x].m.x], so only the Hermitian part (m + ConjugateTranspose[m])/2 matters (Wolfram); exact matrices use principal minors, machine matrices the eigenvalues of the Hermitian part with a tolerance of n*$MachineEpsilon*max\|eigenvalue\|; False for symbolic matrices unless every sign can be proved, and for non-matrices |
| `NegativeSemidefiniteMatrixQ` | added | 3 | Re[Conjugate[x].m.x] <= 0 for all x; the quadratic form is Re[Conjugate[x].m.x], so only the Hermitian part (m + ConjugateTranspose[m])/2 matters (Wolfram); exact matrices use principal minors, machine matrices the eigenvalues of the Hermitian part with a tolerance of n*$MachineEpsilon*max\|eigenvalue\|; False for symbolic matrices unless every sign can be proved, and for non-matrices |
| `NormalMatrixQ` | added | 4 | m.ConjugateTranspose[m] == ConjugateTranspose[m].m |
| `OrthogonalMatrixQ` | added | 16 | p x q matrix: Transpose[m].m == IdentityMatrix[q] when p >= q, m.Transpose[m] == IdentityMatrix[p] when p <= q (no conjugation, as in Wolfram); options Normalized -> False (only orthogonal columns/rows), Tolerance -> t (infinity norm of the defect), SameTest -> f |
| `Orthogonalize` | added | 9 | Gram-Schmidt orthonormal basis using the standard inner product Conjugate[u].v; the output has as many vectors as the input, linearly dependent vectors become zero vectors; machine vectors whose residual norm is at most 100 $MachineEpsilon (about 2.2*10^-14) of the original are treated as dependent (Tolerance -> t overrides); Orthogonalize[list, f] uses the inner product f on arbitrary elements |
| `Permanent` | added | 5 | sum over permutations of products of entries (SymPy Matrix.per); square matrices |
| `PositiveDefiniteMatrixQ` | added | 12 | Re[Conjugate[x].m.x] > 0 for all non-zero x; the quadratic form is Re[Conjugate[x].m.x], so only the Hermitian part (m + ConjugateTranspose[m])/2 matters (Wolfram); exact matrices use principal minors, machine matrices the eigenvalues of the Hermitian part with a tolerance of n*$MachineEpsilon*max\|eigenvalue\|; False for symbolic matrices unless every sign can be proved, and for non-matrices |
| `PositiveSemidefiniteMatrixQ` | added | 5 | Re[Conjugate[x].m.x] >= 0 for all x; the quadratic form is Re[Conjugate[x].m.x], so only the Hermitian part (m + ConjugateTranspose[m])/2 matters (Wolfram); exact matrices use principal minors, machine matrices the eigenvalues of the Hermitian part with a tolerance of n*$MachineEpsilon*max\|eigenvalue\|; False for symbolic matrices unless every sign can be proved, and for non-matrices |
| `Projection` | added | 8 | Projection[u, v] = (u.Conjugate[v]/v.Conjugate[v]) v; Projection[u, v, f] uses the inner product f[u, v]/f[v, v] v; projection onto the zero vector raises |
| `ReflectionMatrix` | added | 5 | IdentityMatrix[n] - 2 v.ConjugateTranspose[v]/(Conjugate[v].v): reflection in the hyperplane normal to the numeric vector v |
| `RotationMatrix` | added | 16 | RotationMatrix[t] (2D, counterclockwise), RotationMatrix[t, w] (3D, right-handed about the numeric axis w), RotationMatrix[t, {u, v}] (n-D, in the plane of u and v, from u towards v), RotationMatrix[{u, v}] (rotation taking the direction of u to that of v, in their plane; antiparallel vectors raise) |
| `ScalingMatrix` | added | 3 | ScalingMatrix[{s1, ..., sn}] = DiagonalMatrix[{s1, ..., sn}]; ScalingMatrix[s, v] scales by s along the numeric direction v: IdentityMatrix + (s - 1) v.Transpose[v]/v.v |
| `ShearingMatrix` | added | 7 | ShearingMatrix[t, v, n] = IdentityMatrix + Tan[t] Outer[Times, vp, Normalize[n]] where vp is the unit vector along the part of v perpendicular to n (Wolfram uses the projection for non-orthogonal v); determinant 1 |
| `SingularValueList` | added | 12 | non-zero singular values in decreasing order (exact for exact matrices, LAPACK for machine matrices, where values below 100*10^-p times the largest are dropped); SingularValueList[m, k] (k < 0: smallest) and UpTo[k] use all Min[Dimensions[m]] values, zeros included; Tolerance -> t keeps values >= t times the largest; symbolic and generalized {m, a} inputs raise |
| `SparseArray` | added | 27 | SparseArray[{pos -> v, ...}], SparseArray[rules, dims], SparseArray[rules, dims, default] and SparseArray[list] (positions may be patterns such as {i_, i_} -> 1; the first matching rule wins). MikoshiLang has no sparse storage: the result is the equivalent dense array, i.e. Normal[SparseArray[...]] in Wolfram (Head is List, not SparseArray); Band[...] -> v specifications are expanded (see Band) |
| `SquareMatrixQ` | added | 4 | True for a matrix with as many rows as columns, False for anything else |
| `TensorContract` | added | 6 | TensorContract[t, {{s1, s2}, ...}] sums over each pair of slots of an explicit array; the remaining slots keep their order; paired slots must have equal dimensions and appear once |
| `TensorDimensions` | added | 3 | Dimensions of an explicit rectangular array ({} for a number); symbolic tensors stay unevaluated |
| `TensorProduct` | added | 8 | tensor (outer) product of explicit arrays, dimensions concatenated (Outer[Times, a, b] for arrays); numbers act as rank-0 tensors; TensorProduct[t] = t; symbolic factors leave it unevaluated |
| `TensorRank` | added | 4 | the number of indices of an explicit rectangular array (ArrayDepth), 0 for a number; symbols and ragged lists stay unevaluated |
| `TensorTranspose` | added | 4 | TensorTranspose[t, perm] for an explicit array: Transpose[t, perm] with perm a full permutation list of the slots (Cycles specifications are not supported) |
| `ToeplitzMatrix` | added | 6 | ToeplitzMatrix[n] (symmetric, successive integers), ToeplitzMatrix[c] (symmetric, first column c), ToeplitzMatrix[c, r] (first column c, first row r; r[[1]] is ignored and c[[1]] used, as in Wolfram); always a dense matrix (Wolfram's TargetStructure option is not supported) |
| `UnitaryMatrixQ` | added | 5 | as OrthogonalMatrixQ with ConjugateTranspose; options Normalized, Tolerance, SameTest |
| `UpperTriangularize` | added | 5 | zeros below the k-th diagonal (keeps m[[i,j]] with j - i >= k; default k = 0); rectangular matrices |
| `VandermondeMatrix` | added | 5 | rows {1, x_i, x_i^2, ...}: VandermondeMatrix[{x1, ..., xn}] (n x n) and VandermondeMatrix[x, k] (n x k), returned as the dense matrix (Wolfram returns a structured array whose Normal this is); confluent (repeated) nodes raise |

### lists (batch 1)

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `AllMatch` | added | 3 | True if every element (or association value) matches the pattern; operator form |
| `AllTrue` | added | 4 | test[e] is True for every element (stops at the first failure); optional level |
| `AnyMatch` | added | 3 | True if some element matches the pattern; operator form |
| `AnyTrue` | added | 3 | test[e] is True for some element (stops at the first success); optional level |
| `AppendTo` | added | 6 | AppendTo[s, e] sets s = Append[s, e] and returns the new value (s must have a value) |
| `Array` | added | 13 | Array[f, n], Array[f, {n1, n2, ...}], origin r or {r1, ...}, {a, b} ranges and head h |
| `ArrayDepth` | added | 3 | depth to which expr is a full array of lists (0 for non-lists) |
| `ArrayFlatten` | added | 3 | ArrayFlatten[{{m11, m12, ...}, ...}] builds a block matrix; scalar blocks (e.g. 0) become constant blocks of the matching size |
| `ArrayQ` | added | 6 | ArrayQ[expr], ArrayQ[expr, n] (n may be a pattern) and ArrayQ[expr, n, test] for the elements |
| `ArrayReshape` | added | 3 | ArrayReshape[list, dims]: the flattened elements arranged with the given dimensions, padded with 0 (or the given padding) when there are too few |
| `Between` | added | 4 | min <= x <= max for {min, max}, or within any interval of {{min1, max1}, ...}; Between[{min, max}] is an operator form |
| `BinCounts` | added | 6 | counts in bins [b_k, b_k+1): BinCounts[data, dx] (bins at multiples of dx), BinCounts[data, {min, max, dx}] and BinCounts[data, {{b1, b2, ...}}] |
| `BinLists` | added | 3 | the elements falling in each bin [b_k, b_k+1), with the same bin specifications as BinCounts |
| `BlockMap` | added | 3 | BlockMap[f, list, n] = Map[f, Partition[list, n]]; BlockMap[f, list, n, d] |
| `BooleanQ` | promoted | 3 | True exactly for True and False |
| `Catenate` | added | 3 | joins a list of lists (and/or associations, whose values are used) into one list |
| `Comap` | added | 3 | Comap[{f, g, ...}, x] = {f[x], g[x], ...} (any head; associations keep their keys) |
| `ComapApply` | added | 3 | ComapApply[{f, g, ...}, {x, y}] = {f[x, y], g[x, y], ...} |
| `ComposeList` | added | 3 | ComposeList[{f1, f2, ...}, x] = {x, f1[x], f2[f1[x]], ...} |
| `ConstantArray` | added | 8 | ConstantArray[c, n] and ConstantArray[c, {n1, n2, ...}] |
| `Construct` | added | 4 | Construct[f, x, y, ...] = f[x, y, ...] |
| `ContainsAll` | added | 3 | every element of e2 occurs in e1 (SameQ or SameTest) |
| `ContainsAny` | added | 3 | some element of e2 occurs in e1 |
| `ContainsExactly` | added | 4 | e1 and e2 have the same distinct elements |
| `ContainsNone` | added | 3 | no element of e2 occurs in e1 |
| `ContainsOnly` | added | 3 | every element of e1 occurs in e2 |
| `CountDistinct` | added | 3 | number of distinct (SameQ) elements; CountDistinct[list, test] |
| `CountDistinctBy` | added | 3 | number of distinct values of f |
| `Counts` | added | 3 | <\|e -> count, ...\|> in order of first appearance (SameQ) |
| `CountsBy` | added | 3 | <\|f[e] -> count, ...\|> in order of first appearance |
| `Delete` | added | 3 | Delete[expr, n], Delete[expr, {i, j, ...}] and Delete[expr, {{...}, ...}] (positions refer to the original expression) |
| `DeleteAdjacentDuplicates` | added | 3 | removes elements equal (SameQ, or the given test) to their predecessor |
| `DeleteDuplicatesBy` | added | 3 | keeps the first element for each distinct (SameQ) value of f |
| `DeleteElements` | added | 3 | DeleteElements[list, elems] removes every occurrence of the elements; DeleteElements[list, n -> elems] at most n of each |
| `Depth` | added | 4 | 1 for atoms, else 1 + the maximum depth of the elements (heads ignored; an association is one level) |
| `Dimensions` | added | 23 | dimensions of a full array whose levels all have the head of expr (Dimensions[f[a, b]] = {2}, Dimensions[{f[a], f[b]}] = {2}); Dimensions[expr, n] at most n levels |
| `DisjointQ` | added | 3 | the lists have no element in common |
| `DuplicateFreeQ` | added | 3 | no two elements are SameQ (or equivalent under the given test) |
| `Extract` | added | 3 | Extract[expr, pos], Extract[expr, {pos1, pos2, ...}] and Extract[expr, pos, h] |
| `FirstCase` | added | 3 | FirstCase[expr, patt \| patt :> rhs, default, levelspec]; default level {1}; Missing["NotFound"] when nothing matches |
| `FirstPosition` | added | 3 | first position matching patt in Position order; Missing["NotFound"] or the given default; optional level specification |
| `FlattenAt` | added | 3 | FlattenAt[list, n], FlattenAt[list, {i, j, ...}] and several positions {{...}, ...}: splices the sublist at that position into its parent |
| `FoldPair` | added | 3 | the last output of FoldPairList; FoldPair[f, y0, list, g] gives g[last pair] |
| `FoldPairList` | added | 4 | FoldPairList[f, y0, {x1, ...}]: f[y, x] returns {output, new y}; the list of outputs; FoldPairList[f, y0, list, g] gives g[{output, y}] instead; FoldPairList[f, list] starts from the first element |
| `Gather` | promoted | 3 | classes of identical (SameQ) elements in order of first appearance; Gather[list, test] |
| `GatherBy` | added | 3 | classes with identical f values, in order of first appearance; GatherBy[list, {f1, f2}] nests |
| `Insert` | added | 3 | Insert[list, elem, n] (negative n counts from the end: -1 appends), Insert[expr, elem, {i, j, ...}] and several positions {{...}, ...} |
| `IntegerLength` | added | 4 | number of base-b digits of \|n\| (default 10); IntegerLength[0] = 0; threads |
| `IntegerReverse` | added | 4 | the integer with the base-b digits of \|n\| reversed; IntegerReverse[n, b, len] pads to len digits first; threads |
| `IntersectingQ` | added | 3 | the lists have at least one element in common |
| `LeafCount` | added | 7 | number of atomic subexpressions including heads (Rational and Complex count as 3); Heads -> False skips heads |
| `LengthWhile` | added | 3 | number of leading elements for which crit is True |
| `Level` | added | 5 | Level[expr, levelspec] (post-order, as Wolfram; associations are one level of values); Level[expr, spec, f] gives f[parts...]; Heads -> True |
| `LexicographicSort` | added | 4 | sorts expressions by comparing their elements in canonical order, a proper prefix first; LexicographicSort[list, p] uses ordering function p on elements |
| `MapApply` | added | 3 | MapApply[f, expr] = f @@@ expr = Apply[f, expr, {1}] |
| `MapAt` | added | 4 | MapAt[f, expr, n \| {i, j, ...} \| {{...}, ...}]; positions may use negative indices, All, Span and Key; a repeated position applies f repeatedly |
| `MatrixQ` | added | 4 | a list of equal-length lists (depth-2 full array); MatrixQ[expr, test] |
| `MaximalBy` | added | 3 | MaximalBy[list, f]: all elements maximising f (canonical order of f values), in their original order; MaximalBy[list, f, n \| UpTo[n]]: the n largest by f |
| `MinMax` | added | 4 | {Min[list], Max[list]}; MinMax[list, d] = {min - d, max + d}; MinMax[list, {dmin, dmax}]; Scaled[s] pads by s times the range |
| `MinimalBy` | added | 3 | MinimalBy[list, f]: all elements minimising f; MinimalBy[list, f, n]: the n smallest by f |
| `Minus` | added | 6 | Minus[x] = -x (Times[-1, x]); threads over lists |
| `NestWhile` | added | 3 | applies f while test[result] is True; NestWhile[f, x, test, m \| All, max, n] |
| `NestWhileList` | added | 3 | the list of results of NestWhile, starting with x |
| `NoneMatch` | added | 3 | True if no element matches the pattern; operator form |
| `NoneTrue` | added | 3 | test[e] is True for no element; optional level |
| `NumericalSort` | added | 3 | sorts numeric elements by numerical value (ties by canonical order); non-numeric elements raise |
| `Operate` | added | 4 | Operate[p, f[x, y]] = p[f][x, y]; Operate[p, expr, 0] = p[expr]; Operate[p, expr, n] applies p to the head n levels down (f[a][b] at level 2 gives p[f][a][b]); an atomic head that deep leaves expr unchanged |
| `Order` | added | 3 | 1 if e1 comes before e2 in canonical order, -1 if after, 0 if identical |
| `OrderedQ` | added | 5 | True when the elements are in canonical (non-decreasing) order, or ordered by p |
| `Ordering` | added | 5 | positions that put the list in canonical order (stable); Ordering[list, n] the first n (negative n: the last n), Ordering[list, n, p] with ordering p |
| `OrderingBy` | added | 3 | positions that sort the list by f (ties by canonical order of the elements); OrderingBy[list, f, n] |
| `PadLeft` | added | 4 | PadLeft[list, n, x] pads on the left with x (default 0) or keeps the last n elements; PadLeft[list, {n1, n2}] and PadLeft[ragged] (to a full array); cyclic list padding raises |
| `PadRight` | added | 4 | PadRight[list, n, x] pads on the right with x (default 0) or keeps the first n elements; PadRight[list, {n1, n2}] and PadRight[ragged]; cyclic list padding raises |
| `Pick` | added | 3 | Pick[list, sel] keeps elements whose selector is True; Pick[list, sel, patt] those whose selector matches patt; nested selector lists pick at deeper levels |
| `PositionIndex` | added | 3 | <\|e -> {positions}, ...\|> in order of first appearance |
| `PositionLargest` | added | 3 | positions of the largest element(s); PositionLargest[list, n] positions of the n largest in decreasing order |
| `PositionSmallest` | added | 3 | positions of the smallest element(s); PositionSmallest[list, n] |
| `PowerRange` | added | 3 | PowerRange[b] = {1, 10, 100, ...} up to b; PowerRange[a, b] and PowerRange[a, b, r] |
| `PrependTo` | added | 3 | PrependTo[s, e] sets s = Prepend[s, e] and returns the new value |
| `Ratios` | added | 3 | successive ratios a[i+1]/a[i]; Ratios[list, n] for n-th ratios |
| `Reap` | added | 21 | Reap[expr] -> {value, {{sown...} per tag in order of first use}}; Reap[expr, patt] and Reap[expr, {p1, ...}] keep matching tags; Reap[expr, patt, f] applies f[tag, list] |
| `Replace` | added | 7 | Replace[expr, rules] tries the rules on the whole expression only (unlike ReplaceAll); Replace[expr, rules, levelspec] at the given levels, deepest parts first; a list of rule lists gives a list of results |
| `ReplaceList` | added | 5 | all results of applying the rules to the whole expression in every way they match (sequence patterns give one result per split; Orderless heads give each distinct binding once, in matcher order rather than Wolfram's); ReplaceList[expr, rules, n] keeps at most n |
| `ReplacePart` | added | 3 | ReplacePart[expr, pos -> new], lists of rules, lists of positions, position patterns such as {i_, i_} -> 0 and pos :> new (evaluated per position) |
| `ReverseApplied` | added | 3 | ReverseApplied[f][a, b, c] = f[c, b, a] |
| `ReverseSort` | added | 3 | Reverse[Sort[list]] (reverse canonical order); ReverseSort[list, p] |
| `ReverseSortBy` | added | 3 | Reverse[SortBy[list, f]]: decreasing f values, ties in reverse canonical order |
| `Scan` | added | 4 | evaluates f on each element (or each part at the given levels) for its side effects; returns Null (Return[v] inside f returns v) |
| `SelectFirst` | added | 3 | first element with crit[e] True, else Missing["NotFound"] or the default |
| `SequenceCases` | added | 4 | non-overlapping runs matching a list pattern ({a, _}, {__Integer}, {x_, y_} /; cond, ...; the longest match at each start) or patt -> rhs; Overlaps -> True |
| `SequenceCount` | added | 3 | number of non-overlapping occurrences of a sublist or list pattern |
| `SequencePosition` | added | 4 | {start, end} of runs matching a list pattern; overlapping matches are included (Overlaps -> True default); Overlaps -> False |
| `SequenceReplace` | added | 3 | replaces non-overlapping runs matching a list pattern -> rhs (rhs is one element) |
| `SequenceSplit` | added | 3 | splits at runs matching a list pattern (empty pieces at the ends dropped, as Wolfram); patt -> rhs keeps rhs between the pieces |
| `SortBy` | added | 4 | sorts by canonical order of f[e] (ties by the canonical order of e); SortBy[list, {f1, f2, ...}] lexicographic; SortBy[list, f, p] uses ordering p; associations are sorted by value |
| `Sow` | added | 21 | Sow[e] and Sow[e, tag] give e and record it for the innermost enclosing Reap (outside Reap, Sow just returns e) |
| `Split` | added | 3 | runs of identical (SameQ) adjacent elements; Split[list, test] tests adjacent pairs |
| `SplitBy` | added | 3 | runs with identical f values; SplitBy[list, {f1, f2, ...}] splits recursively |
| `Subdivide` | added | 3 | Subdivide[n], Subdivide[xmax, n], Subdivide[xmin, xmax, n]: n + 1 equally spaced values |
| `Subsequences` | added | 4 | contiguous subsequences by length then position; Subsequences[list, n \| {n} \| {m, n}] |
| `TakeDrop` | added | 5 | TakeDrop[list, spec] = {Take[list, spec], Drop[list, spec]} |
| `TakeLargest` | added | 4 | the n largest numeric elements in decreasing order (Missing[...] ignored); UpTo[n]; an association gives an association |
| `TakeLargestBy` | added | 3 | the n elements with the largest numeric f values, in decreasing order of f |
| `TakeList` | added | 3 | TakeList[list, {n1, n2, ...}] gives successive pieces; ni may be UpTo[k] or All |
| `TakeSmallest` | added | 3 | the n smallest numeric elements in increasing order (Missing[...] ignored); UpTo[n]; an association gives an association |
| `TakeSmallestBy` | added | 3 | the n elements with the smallest numeric f values, in increasing order of f |
| `TakeWhile` | added | 3 | leading elements for which crit is True |
| `Through` | added | 3 | Through[p[f, g][x]] = p[f[x], g[x]]; Through[expr, h] only when p is h |
| `VectorQ` | added | 4 | a list none of whose elements is a list; VectorQ[expr, test] |

### lists and sets

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Accumulate` | fixed | 3 | exact running totals (legacy converted to floats and rounded to 10 digits); works on symbols |
| `Cardinality` | fixed | 3 | number of distinct elements (legacy returned Length, counting duplicates) |
| `Chunk` | verified | 3 | consecutive blocks of size n, last one possibly shorter (MikoshiLang) |
| `Complement` | fixed | 4 | Complement[all, e1, e2, ...] in canonical order (legacy: 2 arguments only, sorted by string form); SameTest -> f |
| `DeleteDuplicates` | fixed | 4 | SameQ comparison keeping first occurrences (legacy compared string forms, so the string "x" and the symbol x were treated as duplicates); DeleteDuplicates[list, test] |
| `Differences` | fixed | 6 | exact successive differences, Differences[list, n] for n-th differences (legacy used rounded floats) |
| `ElementQ` | fixed | 3 | ElementQ[x, list]: SameQ membership (legacy used ==, so ElementQ[1, {1.0}] was True; MemberQ gives False) |
| `GroupBy` | fixed | 7 | legacy ignored the function and grouped equal values; now GroupBy[list, f] -> <\|k -> {...}\|> keyed by f[elem] in order of first appearance; GroupBy[list, f -> g] maps g over each group's elements, GroupBy[list, spec, red] applies red to each group, GroupBy[list, {f1, f2}] nests; associations group their values |
| `Interleave` | fixed | 3 | {a1, b1, ..., a2, b2, ...} for two or more lists (MikoshiLang); unequal lengths now raise (legacy silently truncated) |
| `Intersection` | fixed | 5 | variadic; canonical order (legacy sorted by string form); SameTest -> f |
| `Partition` | fixed | 7 | Partition[list, n] and Partition[list, n, d] (incomplete blocks dropped, as Wolfram); Partition[list, UpTo[n]] (and UpTo[n], d) keeps a shorter final block; legacy truncated non-integer n |
| `Riffle` | fixed | 3 | Riffle[list, x] and Riffle[list, {y1, ...}] (interleaves when the second list has n or n - 1 elements; legacy inserted the whole list each time) |
| `RotateLeft` | verified | 4 | RotateLeft[list] and RotateLeft[list, n] (n may be negative) |
| `RotateRight` | verified | 4 | RotateRight[list] and RotateRight[list, n] |
| `SetDifference` | fixed | 3 | alias of Complement[a, b] (canonical order) |
| `SlidingWindow` | verified | 3 | overlapping windows = Partition[list, n, 1] (MikoshiLang) |
| `SubsetQ` | fixed | 4 | SubsetQ[l1, l2] is True when l2 is a subset of l1 (Wolfram order; legacy tested the reverse) |
| `SymmetricDifference` | fixed | 3 | elements in exactly one of the two lists, canonical order (legacy sorted by string form) |
| `Tally` | promoted | 4 | {{elem, count}, ...} in order of first appearance (SameQ); Tally[list, test] uses the first representative of each class |
| `Union` | promoted | 9 | variadic, including Union[list]; sorted in canonical order with SameQ duplicates removed; SameTest -> f (legacy 2-argument form sorted by string, so Union[{10, 9}, {}] gave {10, 9}) |

### logic

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `AllSAT` | verified | 3 | all satisfying assignments as rule lists (MikoshiLang) |
| `BooleanMinimize` | verified | 4 | minimal DNF (SymPy simplify_logic, Quine-McCluskey) |
| `CNF` | verified | 3 | conjunctive normal form (SymPy to_cnf; Wolfram BooleanConvert[e, "CNF"]) |
| `DNF` | verified | 3 | disjunctive normal form (SymPy to_dnf) |
| `Equivalent` | fixed | 30 | variadic; True when all arguments are equal booleans; Equivalent[True, a, b] = a && b, Equivalent[False, a] = !a, mixed True/False = False (batch 6) |
| `Iff` | fixed | 4 | alias of Equivalent; legacy used Python bool() so Iff[x, y] was True for any symbols |
| `Implies` | fixed | 18 | Implies[p, q]; Implies[True, q] = q, Implies[False, q] = True, Implies[p, True] = True, Implies[p, False] = !p (batch 6) |
| `Nand` | fixed | 7 | variadic Not[And[...]]; Nand[False, x] = True, Nand[True, x] = !x (batch 6) |
| `Nor` | fixed | 6 | variadic Not[Or[...]]; Nor[True, x] = False, Nor[False, x] = !x (batch 6) |
| `SATSolve` | fixed | 4 | one satisfying assignment {x -> True, ...} (all variables, alphabetical) or False; legacy omitted variables SymPy did not need and could return {True -> True} for tautologies |
| `TruthTable` | verified | 3 | rows {v1, ..., vn, value} over all assignments, variables in alphabetical order (MikoshiLang) |
| `Xnor` | fixed | 8 | Not[Xor[...]] with the same partial evaluation as Xor (batch 6) |
| `Xor` | fixed | 35 | variadic exclusive or; True/False arguments are simplified away (Xor[True, c] = !c, Xor[False, c] = c; batch 6) |

### logic (batch 6)

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Boole` | added | 6 | Boole[True] = 1, Boole[False] = 0; other arguments stay unevaluated |
| `BooleanConvert` | added | 22 | forms "DNF"/"SOP" (default), "CNF"/"POS" (minimal two-level forms), "ANF" (unique algebraic normal form, Xor of Ands), "ESOP" (given as the ANF, which is an exclusive sum of products), "NAND", "NOR", "AND", "OR" (two-level with Not); BooleanConvert[expr, form, cond] uses cond as a care set for DNF/CNF; other forms (IMPLIES, ITE, BFF, BDT, binary NAND/NOR) raise |
| `BooleanCountingFunction` | added | 7 | BooleanCountingFunction[kmax, n] (at most kmax True), [{k}, n] (exactly k), [{k1, k2, ...}, n], [{{kmin, kmax}}, n] pure functions; with a variable list gives an equivalent minimal DNF |
| `BooleanFunction` | added | 18 | BooleanFunction[k, n] pure function (truth table IntegerDigits[k, 2, 2^n] in Tuples[{True, False}, n] order; BooleanFunction[8, 2] is And) applied to True/False arguments; BooleanFunction[k, {vars}] and BooleanFunction[table, {vars}] give an equivalent minimal disjunctive normal form; BooleanFunction[table] gives BooleanFunction[k, n] |
| `BooleanTable` | added | 23 | truth values over Tuples[{True, False}, n] (BooleanTable[expr] uses BooleanVariables); nested tables for several variable lists (outermost first); pure BooleanFunction / BooleanCountingFunction / Function; non-Boolean expressions are substituted (BooleanTable[f[a, b], {a, b}]) |
| `BooleanVariables` | added | 5 | the distinct variables (non-connective atoms) of a Boolean expression in canonical order |
| `Conjunction` | added | 3 | Conjunction[expr, {a1, ...}]: And of expr over all True/False values of the a_i, as an equivalent minimal DNF |
| `Disjunction` | added | 3 | Disjunction[expr, {a1, ...}]: Or of expr over all True/False values of the a_i, as an equivalent minimal DNF |
| `Exists` | added | 9 | Exists[x, expr] and Exists[x, cond, expr] (x a symbol or list of symbols); evaluates when the body does not depend on the variables (e.g. Exists[x, True] = True), otherwise stays for Resolve |
| `ForAll` | added | 11 | ForAll[x, expr] and ForAll[x, cond, expr] (x a symbol or list of symbols); evaluates when the body does not depend on the variables (e.g. ForAll[x, True] = True), otherwise stays for Resolve |
| `LogicalExpand` | added | 8 | expands Boolean connectives into a disjunction of conjunctions (distributing And over Or, pushing Not inwards, expanding Xor/Implies/Equivalent), removing contradictory and absorbed terms; equations between lists are split elementwise; non-Boolean atoms (equations, inequalities) are kept as they are (LogicalExpand[x y == 0] is not split into cases) |
| `Majority` | added | 14 | True when more than half of the (odd number of) arguments are True, False when more than half are False; symbolic arguments stay unevaluated unless the count is already decided; an even number of arguments raises |
| `Resolve` | added | 12 | eliminates ForAll/Exists from purely Boolean formulas (variables over Booleans) and returns an equivalent minimal DNF; quantifiers over non-Boolean formulas (real/complex/integer quantifier elimination) raise; quantifier-free input is returned unchanged |
| `SatisfiabilityCount` | added | 9 | number of assignments (of BooleanVariables or the given variables, so extra variables double the count) that make the expression True |
| `SatisfiabilityInstances` | added | 8 | SatisfiabilityInstances[expr, vars], [expr, vars, m], [expr, vars, All] and pure-function forms: lists of truth values that make expr True, enumerated with False before True (lexicographic); {} if unsatisfiable. Wolfram does not document which instances it returns first; every returned instance is valid |
| `SatisfiableQ` | added | 6 | True if some assignment of the variables makes the expression True (exact truth table up to 22 variables, SymPy SAT solver beyond) |
| `TautologyQ` | added | 22 | True if every assignment of the variables makes the expression True |

### messages, exceptions & testing (W02)

| Function | Status | Cases | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `$Aborted` | added | 4 | inert symbol: the result of an aborted computation (MemoryConstrained, TimeConstrained, CheckAbort); FailureQ[$Aborted] is True |
| `$AssertFunction` | added | 5 | if set, $AssertFunction[HoldComplete[Assert[test, ...]]] is evaluated when an enabled assertion fails, instead of issuing Assert::asrtf |
| `$Canceled` | added | 3 | inert symbol for a canceled input; Confirm treats it as a failure |
| `$Failed` | added | 12 | inert symbol returned when an operation cannot be done; FailureQ[$Failed] is True |
| `$MessageGroups` | added | 3 | {"name" :> {s::t, ...}, ...} named message groups used by On, Off, Quiet and Check (assignable; the predefined groups list the messages MikoshiLang can issue) |
| `$MessageList` | added | 5 | list of HoldForm[s::t] for messages printed in the session ({} initially; assignable and Block-able; Quiet-ed and switched-off messages are not added) |
| `$MessagePrePrint` | added | 3 | Automatic by default; if set to a function f, message arguments are rendered as f[HoldForm[arg]] |
| `$Messages` | added | 4 | output channels for messages: {OutputStream["stdout", 1]} by default; assign {} to silence messages or OutputStream["stderr", 2] to redirect them |
| `$NewMessage` | added | 3 | if set, $NewMessage[s, "t"] is evaluated when the text of s::t is not defined, and the lookup is retried |
| `$TestFileName` | added | 4 | "" interactively; the absolute file name while TestReport[file] runs the file's tests |
| `$TimedOut` | added | 3 | inert symbol for an operation that timed out |
| `Abort` | added | 10 | Abort[] aborts the computation: it raises AbortSignal ($Aborted), caught by CheckAbort; inside AbortProtect the abort takes effect when the AbortProtect finishes |
| `AbortProtect` | added | 3 | AbortProtect[expr] (HoldAll) evaluates expr completely, saving any Abort[] or TimeConstrained / MemoryConstrained abort until it finishes (a CheckAbort inside still catches them) |
| `AbsoluteTiming` | added | 4 | AbsoluteTiming[expr] (HoldAll) gives {real seconds elapsed while evaluating expr, result} (time.perf_counter) |
| `Assert` | added | 5 | Assert[test] / Assert[test, tag] (HoldAll): disabled by default (returns Null without evaluating test); after On[Assert] a test that is not True issues Assert::asrtf (Assert::asrtfl for a {line, file} tag) or evaluates $AssertFunction[HoldComplete[Assert[...]]] when that is set; returns Null |
| `ByteCount` | added | 4 | ByteCount[expr] gives the bytes MikoshiLang uses to store expr (Python object sizes, every part counted separately, no sharing); symbols (including True, False, Null) are shared and count 0. Values differ from Wolfram's own memory layout, as the documentation allows between systems |
| `CatchExceptions` | added | 8 | CatchExceptions[expr, spec] (HoldFirst) returns expr's value, or for a thrown exception matching spec (All, tag, {tags}, tag -> handler, {tagspec -> handler, ...}; first matching clause; subtypes match their parents) handler[exceptionData] or, without a handler, the exception's Failure; other exceptions propagate. The operator form CatchExceptions[spec][expr] holds expr |
| `Check` | added | 20 | Check[expr, failexpr] / Check[expr, failexpr, msgs] (HoldAll): returns failexpr if expr generated a matching message that is not switched off and not quieted inside the Check; a raised MikoshiLang error counts as the message f::evalerr |
